Finite-time singularity formation for the heat flow of the H H -system
Yannick Sire, Juncheng Wei, Youquan Zheng, Yifu Zhou

Abstract
. We construct the first example of finite time blow-up solutions for the heat flow of the H H -system, describing the evolution of surfaces with constant mean curvature { u t = Δ ​ u − 2 ​ u x 1 ∧ u x 2 in ​ ℝ 2 × ℝ + , u ⁡ ( ⋅ , 0 ) = u 0 in ​ ℝ 2 , \begin{cases}u_{t}=\Delta u-2u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}~&\text{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\\ u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}~&\text{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\end{cases} where u u : ℝ 2 × ℝ + → ℝ 3 {\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3} . The singularity at finite time forms as a scaled least energy H H -bubble, denoted as W W , exhibiting type II blow-up speed. One key observation is that the linearized operators around W W projected onto W ⟂ W^{\perp} and in the W W -direction are in fact decoupled. On W ⟂ W^{\perp} , the linearization is the linearized harmonic map heat flow, while in the W W -direction, it is the linearized Liouville-type flow. Based on this, we also prove the non-degeneracy of the H H -bubbles with any degree.



1.
Introduction and main results

A classical problem in geometric analysis is the following
Plateau
problem: for a given curve
\Gamma
, find a surface with boundary
\Gamma
, with mean curvature
H(x)
for a point
x
on the surface, where
H
is some (smooth) function. In the case of the ambient space
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, a parametric surface with prescribed mean curvature, satisfies the following equation, also known as the
H
-surface system

(1.1)

\Delta u-2H(u)u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}=0~\mbox{ on }D,

where
u
:
D\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with
D
being the unit disk,
H
is a given scalar, “
\wedge
” denotes the wedge product, and, for instance,
u_{x_{1}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}u
. The geometric significance of system (
1.1
) is that conformal solutions
u
, i.e. solutions satisfying additionally,

|u_{x_{1}}|^{2}-|u_{x_{2}}|^{2}=u_{x_{1}}\cdot u_{x_{2}}=0~\mbox{ on }D,

parameterize immersed 2D disk-type surfaces of prescribed mean curvature
H
. Solutions of (
1.1
) may arise as “soap bubbles”, that is, surfaces of least area enclosing a given volume. Concerning the existence and optimal estimates, the Dirichlet problem of the
H
-system was studied intensively in many seminal works, such as Heinz
[
26
]
, Hildebrandt
[
28
,
29
]
, Gulliver-Spruck
[
24
,
25
]
, Steffen
[
47
,
46
]
and Wente
[
56
]
. Struwe
[
50
]
considered the Plateau problem of the
H
-system and proved its existence; see also Duzaar-Steffen
[
22
]
. For more geometric motivations and backgrounds, we refer to the comprehensive monographs of Struwe
[
51
,
52
]
, Duzaar-Steffen
[
21
]
, Steffen
[
49
]
, Bethuel-Caldiroli-Guida
[
2
]
and their references.

For the Dirichlet problem in a smooth bounded domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

(1.2)

\begin{cases}\Delta u=H(x,u,\nabla u)u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}~&\text{ in }~\Omega,\\
u(\cdot)=\tilde{g}~&\text{ on }~\partial\Omega\end{cases}

with the scalar
H
being smooth, it was proved by Wente
[
57
]
and Chanillo-Malchiodi
[
13
]
that there is no nonzero solution in simply connected domain when
\tilde{g}=0
. For
H(x,u,\nabla u)=2
and
\|\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}<1
, a solution with minimal energy was constructed by Hildebrandt in
[
29
]
, while in Brezis-Coron
[
6
]
, Steffen
[
48
]
, Struwe
[
51
]
, the authors considered large energy solutions, and it was proved by Heinz
[
27
]
that the condition
\|\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}<1
is sharp. For general
H(x,u,\nabla u)
, the existence of solutions was proved in a series of works by Caldiroli-Musina
[
8
,
7
,
9
,
39
]
via the variational perturbative method introduced by Ambrosetti and Badiale in
[
1
]
. Furthermore, bubbling and multi-bubble solutions have been constructed in Caldiroli-Musina

[
10
]
and Chanillo-Malchiodi
[
13
]
. Regularity of weak solutions was studied by Musina
[
40
]
. The asymptotic behavior of the solutions for (
1.2
) was studied, for instance, in Sasahara

[
44
]
, Isobe
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
, Caldiroli-Musina
[
11
,
12
]
and the references therein.

Rivière proved in the important work
[
42
]
that two-dimensional conformally invariant nonlinear elliptic PDEs, including the prescribed mean curvature equation and harmonic map equation, can be written in terms of suitable conservation laws. Based on this special compensated-compactness structure, he proved that the solutions of the prescribed bounded mean curvature equation and the harmonic map equation in any manifolds are continuous, and that critical points of two-dimensional continuously differentiable conformally invariant elliptic Lagrangians of the form

(1.3)

E[u]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}dx_{1}\wedge dx_{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(u)(u_{x_{1}},u_{x_{2}})dx_{1}\wedge\;dx_{2}

are continuous. Here
\omega
is a
C^{1}
differential two-form on a
C^{2}
-submanifolds
N^{k}
of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
k
and
m
are arbitrary integers satisfying
1\leq k\leq m
.

For the
H
-system in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with constant mean curvature
H=1
, finite-energy entire solutions were classified in the classical paper
[
5
]
by Brezis-Coron as

u(z)=\Pi\left(\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}\right)+C,\quad z=(x,y)=x+iy

where
\Pi:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the inverse stereographic projection defined by

\Pi(z)=\frac{1}{1+|z|^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}2z\\
|z|^{2}-1\end{bmatrix},

P
,
Q
are polynomials and
C
is a constant vector in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
From this result we know that a typical class of solutions to

(1.4)

\Delta u-2u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}=0~\mbox{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

are
W^{(m)}(z)=\Pi\left(z^{m}\right)
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, where
m
is the degree of the map. Here we consider the so called non-degenerate property of
the degree
m
bubble

W^{(m)}
, which concerns the bounded kernel functions of the linearized equation around
W^{(m)}
as follows

(1.5)

\displaystyle\Delta\phi=2\left(W^{(m)}_{x_{1}}\wedge\phi_{x_{2}}+\phi_{x_{1}}\wedge W^{(m)}_{x_{2}}\right).

The non-degeneracy plays an important role in the construction of
H
-bubbles; see Caldiroli-Musina
[
10
]
for instance. In the polar coordinates,
W^{(m)}
can be written as

W^{(m)}(x)=W^{(m)}(r,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}e^{im\theta}\sin(w_{m})\\
\cos(w_{m})\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{2r^{m}\cos(m\theta)}{r^{2m}+1}\\
\frac{2r^{m}\sin(m\theta)}{r^{2m}+1}\\
\frac{r^{2m}-1}{r^{2m}+1}\end{bmatrix},\quad x=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

and

w_{m}=\pi-2\arctan(r^{m}).

The linearized equation (
1.5
) then becomes

(1.6)

\displaystyle\Delta\phi=\frac{2}{r}\left(W^{(m)}_{r}\wedge\phi_{\theta}+\phi_{r}\wedge W^{(m)}_{\theta}\right)

for
\phi=\phi(r,\theta)
. For
m=\pm 1
, Chanillo and Malchiodi
[
13
]
proved the non-degeneracy result; see also
[
44
,
32
,
33
,
10
,
39
]
and the references therein. Chanillo and Malchiodi further conjectured such non-degeneracy holds true for
the degree
m
bubble
(
|m|\geq 2
). Our first result confirms this:

Theorem 1.1

(Non-degeneracy of
H
-bubbles)
.

The solution to (
1.4
)

W^{(m)}(x)=W^{(m)}(r,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{2r^{m}\cos(m\theta)}{r^{2m}+1}\\
\frac{2r^{m}\sin(m\theta)}{r^{2m}+1}\\
\frac{r^{2m}-1}{r^{2m}+1}\end{bmatrix},\quad x=(r\cos\theta,~r\sin\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}

is non-degenerate in the sense that all bounded solutions of the linearized equation (
1.6
) are linear combinations of
4m+5
functions defined as follows,

(1.7)

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2m}-1}{r^{2m}+1}W^{(m)},\quad\frac{r^{m}}{1+r^{2m}}\cos(m\theta)W^{(m)},\quad\frac{r^{m}}{(1+r^{2m})}\sin(m\theta)W^{(m)},

\displaystyle\frac{r^{m-k}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(\cos(k\theta)E_{1}^{(m)}+\sin(k\theta)E_{2}^{(m)}\right),\quad\frac{r^{m-k}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(\sin(k\theta)E_{1}^{(m)}-\cos(k\theta)E_{2}^{(m)}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{r^{m+l}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(\cos(l\theta)E_{1}^{(m)}-\sin(l\theta)E_{2}^{(m)}\right),\quad\frac{r^{m+l}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(\sin(l\theta)E_{1}^{(m)}+\cos(l\theta)E_{2}^{(m)}\right)

for
k=0,1,\cdots,m
and
l=1,\cdots,m
. Here

E_{1}^{(m)}=E_{1}^{(m)}(r,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{r^{2m}-1}{r^{2m}+1}\cos(m\theta)\\
\frac{r^{2m}-1}{r^{2m}+1}\sin(m\theta)\\
\frac{-2r^{m}}{r^{2m}+1}\end{bmatrix},\quad E_{2}^{(m)}=E_{2}^{(m)}(r,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}-\sin(m\theta)\\
\cos(m\theta)\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

Remark 1.1.1
.

For
m\leq-1
,
W^{(m)}(z)=\Pi^{\prime}\left({\bar{z}}^{|m|}\right)
with

\Pi^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{1+|z|^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}2z\\
1-|z|^{2}\end{bmatrix},

and the same non-degeneracy result holds with
m
replaced by
|m|
.

Note that the method in
[
13
]
for the case
m=\pm 1
depends on the spectrum of
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
and the spherical decomposition on
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. In
[
39
]
, Musina further studied the role of the spectrum of the Laplace operator on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
in the
H
-bubble problem. Here our proof of Theorem
1.1
is based on a decoupling property of the linearized operator and an ODE argument.

A key observation we make is that the linearized equations on
[W^{(m)}]^{\perp}
and in the
W^{(m)}
-direction are decoupled, see the decompositions in Section
3
and Lemma
2.1
(for
m=1
). On
[W^{(m)}]^{\perp}
, the linearized problem can be solved in a similar manner as the one for harmonic maps, see
[
14
, Corollary 1]
. Indeed, the kernel functions (
1.7
) in Theorem
1.1
are the same as those for the linearized harmonic map equation except for the first three functions parallel to
W^{(m)}
at each point. Due to the critical growth in the nonlinearity, the
H
-system (
1.4
) shares some similarities with the harmonic map equation with target manifold being
\mathbb{S}^{2}

\Delta u+|\nabla u|^{2}u=0~\mbox{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

In fact, (
1.4
) is the Euler-Lagrange equation of the energy functional

(1.8)

E_{H}[u]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}u\cdot(u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}):=E_{D}[u]+E_{V}[u],

that is scaling invariant. Here,
E_{D}[u]
is the usual Dirichlet energy that appears in the context of harmonic maps, and
E_{V}[u]
may be referred to as the
H
-volume functional of the surface parametrized by
u
.

Non-degeneracy of harmonic maps proved in
[
14
]
is a rather important property in the study of singularity formation for the harmonic map heat flow and the quantitative stability of harmonic maps, see
[
17
,
18
]
and the references therein. In a related context, we refer the interested readers to
[
38
,
45
,
19
]
for the non-degeneracy of half harmonic maps. Inspired by the works
[
14
,
18
]
on harmonic maps, it might be natural to expect the non-degeneracy of general bubble
u(z)=\Pi\left(\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}\right)+C
and the locally quantitative stability; see also
[
43
]
.

The previous discussion, though important for our purposes, concerns the stationnary problem. In this paper, we focus on the geometric flow that describes the evolution of parametric surfaces with constant mean curvature. It is the associated heat flow of the
H
-system

(1.9)

\begin{cases}u_{t}=\Delta u-2u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}~&\text{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\\
u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}~&\text{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\end{cases}

where
u(x,t)=u(x_{1},x_{2},t):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and
u_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is a given smooth map. The system (
1.9
) is the negative
L^{2}
-gradient flow of the energy (
1.8
), and its stationary equation, namely
H
-system, is the equation satisfied by surfaces of mean curvature
H=1
in conformal representation.
Global existence and regularity for weak solutions of the initial-boundary value problem to the heat flow of the
H
-system were established by Rey in
[
41
]
. Partial regularity of weak solution was studied in the works of Wang
[
53
,
54
]
. Existence and uniqueness of short time regular solution were proved by Chen-Levine
[
15
]
, where they also analyzed the bubbling phenomenon at the first singular time. In
[
3
,
4
]
, Bögelein-Duzaar-Scheven showed short-time regularity, and the global existence of weak solution which is regular except from finitely many singular times; see Duzaar-Scheven
[
20
]
for the global existence for a Plateau problem.

We are interested in the formation of singularity at finite time for the heat flow (
1.9
). Even though it is known that the heat flow for
H
-system shares many similarities with harmonic map flows (and there are now plenty of examples of finite time singularities for harmonic map flows, e.g.
[
17
]
and references therein), there is no example of solutions to (
1.9
) which exhibit singularity formation at finite time, even in the equivariant case. The aim of this paper is to investigate the blow-up mechanism of the system (
1.9
) and its precise asymptotics, and we will prove the first instance of finite time blowup for (
1.9
). Though the elliptic theory for such systems share many similarities with the one for harmonic maps, the geometric flow exhibits behaviour which are surprising and make the analysis substantially harder. As alluded before the splitting of the linearization between directions behaving like the linearization of the harmonic map heat flow around a bubble and the one sharing strong similarities with a Liouville flow (i.e. an exponential nonlinearity) introduces major adjustments to the general strategy due to interaction issues. This behaviour at the linear level, though critical, is nevertheless decoupled which allows to close the argument.

The building block of our construction is the following least energy entire, nontrivial
H
-bubble (
m=1
) given by

(1.10)

W(x)=\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\left[\begin{matrix}2x\\
|x|^{2}-1\end{matrix}\right],\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

where we write
W=W^{(1)}
,
E_{1}=E_{1}^{(1)}
and
E_{2}=E_{2}^{(1)}
for simplicity.
W
satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla W|^{2}=8\pi,\quad W(\infty)=(0,0,1)^{\mathsf{T}}.

Note that
W(x)
can be expressed as the
1
-equivariant form

(1.11)

W(x)=\left[\begin{matrix}e^{i\theta}\sin w(r)\\
\cos w(r)\end{matrix}\right],\,\,w(r)=\pi-2\arctan(r),\,\,x=re^{i\theta}.

Define
\gamma
-rotation matrix around
z
-axis as

(1.12)

Q_{\gamma}:=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\gamma&-\sin\gamma&0\\[3.00003pt]
\sin\gamma&\cos\gamma&0\\[3.00003pt]
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}.

Our main result is stated as follows.

Theorem 1.2

(Type II finite-time blow-up)
.

For any point
q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and sufficiently small
T>0
, there exists
u_{0}
such that
\nabla_{x}u(x,t)
with
u(x,t)
solving (
1.9
) blows up at
q
as
t\to T
. More precisely, there exist
\kappa\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
\gamma_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and a map
u_{*}\in H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
such that

u(x,t)-u_{*}(x)-Q_{\gamma_{*}}\left[W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)-W(\infty)\right]\to 0~\text{ as }~t\to T

in
H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
with

\lambda(t)=\kappa\frac{T-t}{|\log(T-t)|^{2}}(1+o(1)),\quad\xi(t)=q+o(1),

where
o(1)\to 0
as
t\to T
. In particular, we have

|\nabla u(\cdot,t)|^{2}dx\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}|\nabla u_{*}|^{2}dx+8\pi\delta_{q}~\text{ as }~t\to T

in the sense of Radon measures.

Remark 1.2.1
.

•

The fine asymptotics of
u
is obtained in the construction:

u(x,t)=Q_{\gamma(t)}W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)+\Phi_{{\rm per}},

where the perturbation
\Phi_{{\rm per}}
is small in the sense that

|\Phi_{{\rm per}}|\lesssim T^{\epsilon_{1}},~|\nabla_{x}\Phi_{{\rm per}}|\lesssim\lambda^{\epsilon_{2}}(t)

for some
\epsilon_{1}>0
and
-1<\epsilon_{2}<0
. More precise asymptotic behavior can be found in Section
8
.

•

The same construction works for the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem in a smooth bounded domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

\begin{cases}u_{t}=\Delta u-2u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}~&\rm{in}~\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\\
u=(0,0,1)^{\mathsf{T}}~&\rm{on}~{\partial}\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\\
u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}~&\rm{in}~\Omega\end{cases}

for given smooth map
u_{0}
with
u_{0}|_{{\partial}\Omega}=(0,0,1)^{\mathsf{T}}
. See
[
30
]
for related existence results as well as blow-up and regularity criteria for (
1.9
) with Dirichlet boundary. It is worth mentioning that with such Dirichlet boundary, the solutions to (
1.9
) are in fact different from those discussed in
[
30
]
, where zero boundary condition was imposed.

•

Motivated by
[
17
]
concerning the blow-up for the harmonic map heat flow, it is reasonable to expect that the finite time singularities might also happen in the gradient flow for the general energy functional (
1.3
), and different blow-up mechanism may arise depending on the 2-form
\omega
presumably.

•

Bubbling solutions taking higher degree profile might be possible by virtue of the non-degeneracy in Theorem
1.1
. However, the construction is more involved due to the presence of more bounded kernel functions for the linearized operator.

The general framework of the construction is based on the inner-outer gluing method, first developed by Cortázar-del Pino-Musso
[
16
]
and Dávila-del Pino-Wei
[
17
]
, and it is a versatile approach designed for the singularity formation for evolution PDEs.

The heat flow of the
H
-system is intimately related to the harmonic map heat flow

u_{t}=\Delta u+|\nabla u|^{2}u~\mbox{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

due to their similar quadratic nonlinearities, and our construction is primarily inspired by Dávila-del Pino-Wei
[
17
]
concerning the blow-up for the latter geometric flow. Despite similar criticality and structure shared with harmonic map heat flow, the blow-up mechanism of (
1.9
) turns out to be more delicate due to the system in the
W
-direction. In fact, as a consequence of Lemma
2.1
, the elliptic linearization decouples as the linearized harmonic map equation on
W^{\perp}
and the linearized Liouville equation in the
W
-direction. Compared to the case of the harmonic map heat flow, the treatment required in the
W
-direction for (
1.9
) results in several technical novelties and a somewhat more unstable blow-up beyond the equivariant symmetry class. We shall describe these in what follows.

We now describe informally our construction. Recall we are interested in constructing solutions without symmetry assumptions. The major difficulties in the construction arise from the non-radial symmetry and the non-local/global effects, triggered by the slow spatial decay, in
both

W
-direction and on
W^{\perp}
. Indeed, the ansatz for the desired blow-up solution with equivariant-type symmetry (
1.13
) turns out to be relatively simpler without the introduction of extra modulation parameters
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
corresponding to the parabolic linearized Liouville equation in the
W
-direction. Due to the non-radial symmetry, the ansatz has to be modified to further improve the slowly decaying errors in non-radial modes, yielding an extra non-local system governing the modes
\pm 1
parameters
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
in the
W
-direction (cf. the
c_{1}
-
c_{2}
system in Section
9
). Moreover, the
c_{1}
-
c_{2}
system is in fact coupled with another
\lambda
-
\gamma
system, governing the scaling parameter
\lambda
and the rotational parameter
\gamma
on
W^{\perp}
in a non-local way, and this is a consequence of slowly decaying error in mode
0
on
W^{\perp}
. The
\lambda
-
\gamma
system was first observed and derived in the context of the harmonic map heat flow
[
17
]
. For (
1.9
), the non-locality of the
c_{1}
-
c_{2}
system and its coupling with the
\lambda
-
\gamma
system seem to be a new feature beyond the symmetry class.

Another aspect is the use of the distorted Fourier transform for the spectral analysis of mode
0
in the
W
-direction (i.e., the linearized Liouville equation, cf. Appendix
B
). The corresponding kernel is of order
1
at space infinity which makes the analysis more subtle, and the techniques for linear theories in all the other modes seem not to be sufficient to ensure a solution with sufficient decay. Instead, we use the techniques of the distorted Fourier transform for this specific mode and carry out a spectral analysis for the associated half-line Schrödinger operator, see Appendix
B
. The motivation is from a series important works of Krieger-Miao-Schlag-Tataru
[
34
,
37
,
35
,
36
]
in the hyperbolic settings and from a recent one
[
55
]
in a dissipative-dispersive setting. These techniques might be of use in the study of semilinear elliptic and parabolic equations with exponential-type nonlinearity.

A roadmap to the construction.

We elaborate in a bit more detailed fashion below. The first step of our proof is to find a good approximate solution. Let us begin with a simple motivational ansatz to solution
u
of (
1.9
), taking the co-rotational form similar to (
1.11
):

(1.13)

u(x,t)=\psi(r,t)\left[\begin{matrix}e^{i\theta}\sin\varphi(r,t)\\
\cos\varphi(r,t)\end{matrix}\right].

Then system (
1.9
) becomes the following system of 1D evolution equations

(1.14)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varphi_{t}=\varphi_{rr}+\frac{\varphi_{r}}{r}+\frac{(-\sin\varphi+2r\varphi_{r})\psi_{r}}{r\psi}-\frac{\sin(2\varphi)}{2r^{2}},\\
&\psi_{t}=\psi_{rr}+\frac{\psi_{r}}{r}-\frac{2\psi^{2}\varphi_{r}\sin\varphi}{r}+\frac{-1+\cos(2\varphi)-2r^{2}\varphi_{r}^{2}}{2r^{2}}\psi.\end{aligned}\right.

One can check directly that
(\pi-2\arctan(r),1)
is a stationary solution. Then a natural approximation solution is

\varphi_{0}(r,t)=\pi-2\arctan\left(\frac{r}{\lambda(t)}\right)\text{ and }\psi_{0}(r,t)=1.

The linearized system of (
1.14
) around
\left(\varphi_{0},~\psi_{0}\right)
is given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\phi_{1})_{t}=(\phi_{1})_{rr}+\frac{(\phi_{1})_{r}}{r}-\frac{r^{4}-6r^{2}\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{4}}{r^{2}(r^{2}+\lambda^{2})^{2}}\phi_{1}-\frac{6\lambda}{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}}(\phi_{2})_{r},\\
&(\phi_{2})_{t}=(\phi_{2})_{rr}+\frac{(\phi_{2})_{r}}{r}+\frac{8\lambda^{2}}{(r^{2}+\lambda^{2})^{2}}\phi_{2}\end{aligned}\right.

for perturbations
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
of
\varphi_{0}
and
\psi_{0}
, respectively. Note the second equation is the linearization for the 2D Liouville equation

\Delta u+e^{u}=0

around the bubble
\log\frac{8\lambda^{2}}{(r^{2}+\lambda^{2})^{2}}
.

Let us emphasize that the ansatz in the motivational example above enjoys the equivariant symmetry, but beyond such class, the ansatz has to be modified significantly due to the non-radial modes. Inspired by the simple ansatz (
1.13
) and the non-degeneracy in Theorem
1.1
, we will find a suitable profile that approximates well the real solution to (
1.9
) and then further improve it by adding several corrections. First we define the error of
u
as

S[u]:=-u_{t}+\Delta u-2u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}.

We take the approximate solution as

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}

\displaystyle:=~Q_{\gamma(t)}\left[W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)+c_{1}(t)\frac{2\rho}{\rho^{2}+1}\cos\theta W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)+c_{2}(t)\frac{2\rho}{\rho^{2}+1}\sin\theta W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle:=~Q_{\gamma}(W+c_{1}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}+c_{2}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}),

where
Q_{\gamma}
is given in (
1.12
), and the modulation parameters
\lambda(t),~\xi(t),~\gamma(t),~c_{1}(t),~c_{2}(t)
are to be adjusted. The most notable difference in the ansatz compared to the radial case is the introduction of the new parameters
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, which in fact correspond to the modes
\pm 1
in the linearized Liouville problem in the
Q_{\gamma}W
-direction. Indeed,
\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}
are the corresponding kernels given in (
2.2
).

As we will see in Section
4
, the non-radial ansatz
U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}
generates slowly decaying errors in both
W
-direction and on
W^{\perp}
, and thus needs to be improved by adding several non-local corrections. We choose the corrected approximate solution as

\displaystyle U_{*}:=

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}+\eta_{1}\Big(\Phi^{(0)}+\Phi^{(1)}+\Phi^{(2)}_{U^{\perp}}+\Phi^{(-2)}_{U^{\perp}}+\Phi^{(2)}_{U}\Big),

where the purpose of the cut-off function

\eta_{1}:=\eta(x-\xi(t))

is to avoid potential slow spatial decay in the remote region, and the five corrections all have their own role. Here the corrections
\Phi^{(0)}
and
\Phi^{(1)}
, in non-local form, are to improve slow spatial decay in mode
0
on
[Q_{\gamma}W]^{\perp}
and in modes
\pm 1
in the
Q_{\gamma}W
-direction. The other three corrections
\Phi^{(\pm 2)}_{U^{\perp}}
,
\Phi^{(2)}_{U}
are to improve slow time decay in modes
\pm 2
on
[Q_{\gamma}W]^{\perp}
and in mode
2
in the
Q_{\gamma}W
-direction, respectively. Such bulky ansatz is necessary when designing weighted spaces for the gluing process, especially in the choice of constants measuring these spaces that the desired solutions reside in (see (
8.29
) in Section
8
). In other words, without these corrections, weighted spaces for the perturbation cannot be found in the current set-up. This is one of the most important parts in the construction, and we do not know if there are simpler ansatzes for the approximate solution.

We next look for a solution with the following form

u=U_{*}+\Phi,

so
S[u]=0
yields

{\partial}_{t}\Phi=\Delta\Phi-2{\partial}_{x_{1}}U_{*}\wedge{\partial}_{x_{2}}\Phi-2{\partial}_{x_{1}}\Phi\wedge{\partial}_{x_{2}}U_{*}-2{\partial}_{x_{1}}\Phi\wedge{\partial}_{x_{2}}\Phi+S[U_{*}].

To implement the inner-outer gluing procedure, we decompose
\Phi
into

\Phi(x,t)=\eta_{R}Q_{\gamma}\Big[\Phi_{W}\left(y,t\right)+\Phi_{W^{\perp}}\left(y,t\right)\Big]+\Psi(x,t),\quad y=\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)},

where
\Phi_{W}
is in the
W
-direction,
\Phi_{W^{\perp}}
is on
W^{\perp}
,

\eta_{R}:=\eta\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{R(t)\lambda(t)}\right),

and
R(t)=\lambda^{-\beta}(t)
with
\beta>0
to be chosen later.
Then it is sufficient to find a desired solution
u
to (
1.9
) if the triple
(\Phi_{W},~\Phi_{W^{\perp}},~\Psi)
satisfies the coupled gluing system:

(1.15)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}{\partial}_{t}\Phi_{W}=

\displaystyle~\Delta_{y}\Phi_{W}-2{\partial}_{y_{1}}W\wedge{\partial}_{y_{2}}\Phi_{W}-2{\partial}_{y_{1}}\Phi_{W}\wedge{\partial}_{y_{2}}W+{\rm RHS}_{{\rm in},W}\quad\mbox{ in }~~\mathcal{D}_{2R},

(1.16)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}{\partial}_{t}\Phi_{W^{\perp}}=

\displaystyle~\Delta_{y}\Phi_{W^{\perp}}-2{\partial}_{y_{1}}W\wedge{\partial}_{y_{2}}\Phi_{W^{\perp}}-2{\partial}_{y_{1}}\Phi_{W^{\perp}}\wedge{\partial}_{y_{2}}W+{\rm RHS}_{{\rm in},W^{\perp}}\quad\mbox{ in }~~\mathcal{D}_{2R},

(1.17)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\Psi=

\displaystyle~\Delta_{x}\Psi+{\rm RHS}_{{\rm out}}\mbox{ in }~~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times(0,T),

i.e.,
\Phi_{W}
and
\Phi_{W^{\perp}}
satisfy inner problem in the
W
-direction and on
W^{\perp}
, respectively, and
\Psi
solves the outer problem. The detailed form of the right hand sides
{\rm RHS}_{{\rm in},W}
,
{\rm RHS}_{{\rm in},W^{\perp}}
and
{\rm RHS}_{{\rm out}}
with couplings will be given in Section
5
.

To attack the inner-outer gluing system (
1.15
)-(
1.17
), a key property derived in Lemma
2.1
is that the equation (
1.16
) for
\Phi_{W^{\perp}}
is essentially a perturbation of the linearized harmonic map heat flow around the bubble
W
, while the equation (
1.15
) for
\Phi_{W}
is a perturbation of the linearized Liouville-type flow, i.e., linearization of

u_{t}=\Delta u+e^{u}~\mbox{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

around the canonical bubble
\log\frac{8\lambda^{-2}}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}},

and these two linearized operators are in fact decoupled. This remarkable structure allows us to develop the linear theories separately in the
W
-direction and on
W^{\perp}
, and the full nonlinear systems for
\Phi_{W^{\perp}}
and
\Phi_{W}
are weakly coupled provided their weighted spaces are properly designed. We will give the full linear theory in Section
7
, and the gluing system will be solved in Section
8
.

As in the ansatz
U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}
, we introduce two modulation parameters
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
that correspond to the slowly decaying kernels
\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}
(Fourier modes
\pm 1
) for the linearized Liouville operator. The non-local corrections
\Phi^{(0)}
and
\Phi^{(1)}
, taking care of the slowly decaying errors respectively on
[Q_{\gamma}W]^{\perp}
and in the
Q_{\gamma}W
-direction, in turn yield two non-local systems that govern the blow-up dynamics of
\lambda
-
\gamma
and
c_{1}
-
c_{2}
through the orthogonality conditions at corresponding modes. For the
\lambda
-
\gamma
system, the influence of
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
turns out to be a perturbation. However, in the
c_{1}
-
c_{2}
system, the coupling from
\lambda
and
\gamma
in fact serves as one of the leading parts, and these parameters obey

(1.18)

\displaystyle\int_{-T}^{t-\lambda^{2}(t)}\frac{p_{1}(s)}{t-s}ds+\frac{2}{3}\left(\int_{-T}^{t-\lambda^{2}(t)}\frac{{\rm Re}[p_{0}(s)e^{-i\gamma(t)}]}{t-s}ds\right){\mathbf{c}}=f(t),

\displaystyle p_{1}(t)=-2(\lambda{\mathbf{c}})^{\prime},\quad{\mathbf{c}}(t)=c_{1}(t)+ic_{2}(t),\quad p_{0}(t)=-2(\dot{\lambda}+i\lambda\dot{\gamma})e^{i\gamma}

for some
f(t)\to 0
as
t\to T
. Our strategy is to approximate (
1.18
) by the local dynamics (
6.15
), where the second term involving
\mathbf{Z}
can be regarded as the main contribution from the
\lambda
-
\gamma
system, and the role of the initial data can be seen; see Section
6
. We also observe from here that, compared to the one for the heat flow of harmonic map, the blow-up for (
1.9
) seems to be somewhat more unstable beyond equivariant symmetry class due to the restrictive assumptions on the initial data. The solvability of the full problem (
1.18
) is more subtle due to the double integro-differential operators, and its resolution will be done by linearization in Section
9
.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, notations and necessary formulas for the linearized operators will be given. In Section
3
, we prove Theorem
1.1
. This part is of independent interest. In Section
4
, we discuss the approximate solution for the desired blow-up and its improvement by introducing a couple of corrections, where some technical analysis is postponed to Appendix
A
. In Section
5
, we make the final ansatz and formulate the gluing system. Asymptotics of the modulation parameters will be derived in Section
6
. In Section
7
, linear theories for the outer problem and the inner problems in the
W
-direction and on
W^{\perp}
will be developed, where the spectral analysis and the pointwise control for the mode
0
in the
W
-direction via distorted Fourier transform will be carried out in Appendix
B
. The full gluing system will be solved in Section
8
. In Section
9
, the linear theory for the
c_{1}
-
c_{2}
system will be established.

2.
Notations and preliminaries

We first list in this section notations, useful properties and formulas for the linearized operator of the
H
-system around the
H
-bubble. Our building block is

W\left(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\right)
given in (
1.10
) with
y=\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda},

and

E_{1}(y)=\left[\begin{matrix}e^{i\theta}\cos w(\rho)\\
-\sin w(\rho)\end{matrix}\right],\quad E_{2}(y)=\left[\begin{matrix}ie^{i\theta}\\
0\end{matrix}\right]

form a Frenet basis associated to
W
. Here

w(\rho)=\pi-2\arctan(\rho),\quad y=\rho e^{i\theta},

so

w_{\rho}=-\frac{2}{\rho^{2}+1},\quad\sin w=-\rho w_{\rho}=\frac{2\rho}{\rho^{2}+1},\quad\cos w=\frac{\rho^{2}-1}{\rho^{2}+1}.

\bullet

Notations.

•

A map
f
is said to be in the
W
-direction if there exists a scalar
c
such that
f=cW
. Similarly,
f
is said to be on
W^{\perp}
(or
f\in W^{\perp}
) if
f\cdot W=0
.

•

The complex form of a map
f=f_{1}E_{1}+f_{2}E_{2}
on
W^{\perp}
is defined by

(f)_{\mathbb{C}}=f_{1}+if_{2}.

•

We write

f:=\Pi_{W}[f]+\Pi_{W^{\perp}}[f]~\mbox{ for }~\Pi_{W^{\perp}}[f]:=f-(f\cdot W)W.

•

A map
f
is said to be in mode
k
on
W^{\perp}
if
\Pi_{W^{\perp}}[f]
can be written as

\Pi_{W^{\perp}}[f]={\rm Re}(f_{k}(r)e^{ik\theta})E_{1}+{\rm Im}(f_{k}(r)e^{ik\theta})E_{2},\quad x=re^{i\theta}.

•

The notation
(\Pi_{W^{\perp}}[f])_{\mathbb{C},j}
denotes the projection of the complex form of
\Pi_{W^{\perp}}[f]
onto mode
j
.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
t\leq T
and admissiable functions
g(x),h(x,t)
, denote

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\circ g\right)(x,t):=(4\pi t)^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}g(y)dy,

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\bullet h\right)(x,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\left[4\pi(t-s)\right]^{-1}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4(t-s)}}h(y,s)dyds.

•

Denote
\langle y\rangle=\sqrt{1+|y|^{2}}
for any
y\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}
.

•

The symbol
``\,\lesssim\,"
means
``\,\leq\,C\,"
for a positive constant
C
independent of
t
and
T
. Here
C
might be different from line to line.

\bullet

Linearized operator.

The linearization around
W
reads

(2.1)

L_{W}[\varphi]:=\Delta_{y}\varphi-2W_{y_{1}}\wedge\varphi_{y_{2}}-2\varphi_{y_{1}}\wedge W_{y_{2}},

and as a consequence of the non-degeneracy of degree 1 map
W
proved by Chanillo-Malchiodi
[
13
]
, all bounded kernel functions of the operator
L_{W}
must be linear combinations of

(2.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &Z_{0,1}(y)=\rho w_{\rho}E_{1}(y),\\
&Z_{0,2}(y)=\rho w_{\rho}E_{2}(y),\\
&Z_{1,1}(y)=w_{\rho}[\cos\theta E_{1}(y)+\sin\theta E_{2}(y)],\\
&Z_{1,2}(y)=w_{\rho}[\sin\theta E_{1}(y)-\cos\theta E_{2}(y)],\\
&Z_{-1,1}(y)=\rho^{2}w_{\rho}[\cos\theta E_{1}(y)-\sin\theta E_{2}(y)],\\
&Z_{-1,2}(y)=\rho^{2}w_{\rho}[\sin\theta E_{1}(y)+\cos\theta E_{2}(y)],\\
&\mathcal{Z}_{0}=\cos wW,\\
&\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}=\cos\theta\sin wW,\\
&\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}=\sin\theta\sin wW.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

For

U:=Q_{\gamma}W,

we also define

L_{U}[\phi]:=\Delta_{x}\phi-2U_{x_{1}}\wedge\phi_{x_{2}}-2\phi_{x_{1}}\wedge U_{x_{2}}.

Here,
Q_{\gamma}
is defined in (
1.12
). Clearly, one has

L_{U}[Q_{\gamma}\varphi]=\lambda^{-2}Q_{\gamma}L_{W}[\varphi].

We now give several useful formulations of
L_{W}
acting on
\varphi
in different forms.

Lemma 2.1
.

If we set

\Phi(y)=\phi_{1}(\rho,\theta)E_{1}+\phi_{2}(\rho,\theta)E_{2}+\phi_{3}(\rho,\theta)W

and suppose that
L_{W}[\Phi]=0
, then the scalars
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{3}
should satisfy the following equations

(2.3)

\displaystyle~\partial_{\rho\rho}\phi_{1}+\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\phi_{1}+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi_{1}-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\phi_{1}+\frac{8}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}}\phi_{1}-\frac{2(\rho^{2}-1)}{\rho^{2}(\rho^{2}+1)}{\partial}_{\theta}\phi_{2}=0,

(2.4)

\displaystyle~\partial_{\rho\rho}\phi_{2}+\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\phi_{2}+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi_{2}-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\phi_{2}+\frac{8}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}}\phi_{2}+\frac{2(\rho^{2}-1)}{\rho^{2}(\rho^{2}+1)}{\partial}_{\theta}\phi_{1}=0,

(2.5)

\displaystyle~\partial_{\rho\rho}\phi_{3}+\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\phi_{3}+\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi_{3}+\frac{8}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}}\phi_{3}=0.

Remark 2.1.1
.

•

We observe from above Lemma that the the components in
W
-direction and on
W^{\perp}
are in fact decoupled under linearization.

•

Notice that the scalar equation (
2.5
) can be regarded as the linearization of the Liouville equation

\Delta u+e^{u}=0~\mbox{ in }~{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

around
\log\frac{8}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}},

which is non-degenerate in the sense that the linearization only has following bounded kernels

\frac{\rho^{2}-1}{\rho^{2}+1},\quad\frac{2\rho e^{i\theta}}{\rho^{2}+1}.

See also (
2.2
).

The next three lemmas concern the expansion of

\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]:=-2U_{x_{1}}\wedge\Phi_{x_{2}}-2\Phi_{x_{1}}\wedge U_{x_{2}}

in the linearization
L_{U}[\Phi]
, and these will be useful in analyzing the couplings in the gluing system.

Lemma 2.2
.

In the polar coordinate system

\Phi(x)=\Phi(r,\theta),\quad x=re^{i\theta},\quad\rho=\frac{r}{\lambda},

the term

\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]

can be expressed as

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\left(\Phi_{r}\cdot(Q_{\gamma}W)\right)Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\frac{2}{\lambda r}w_{\rho}(\Phi_{\theta}\cdot(Q_{\gamma}W))Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\lambda r}w_{\rho}\left[r(\Phi_{r}\cdot(Q_{\gamma}E_{1}))-(\Phi_{\theta}\cdot(Q_{\gamma}E_{2}))\right]Q_{\gamma}W.

We consider a
C^{1}
function
\Phi:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
, that we express in the form

\Phi(x)=\left[\begin{matrix}\varphi_{1}(x)+i\varphi_{2}(x)\\
\varphi_{3}(x)\end{matrix}\right].

We also denote

\varphi=\varphi_{1}+i\varphi_{2},\quad\bar{\varphi}=\varphi_{1}-i\varphi_{2}

and define the operators

\mathop{\rm div}\varphi=\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi_{1}+\partial_{x_{2}}\varphi_{2},\quad\text{curl }\varphi=\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi_{2}-\partial_{x_{2}}\varphi_{1}.

Lemma 2.3
.

In the polar coordinate system

\Phi(x)=\Phi(r,\theta)=(\varphi_{1},~\varphi_{2},~\varphi_{3})^{T},\quad x=re^{i\theta},\quad\rho=\frac{r}{\lambda},

the term
\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]
can be decomposed as follows

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]_{0}+\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]_{1}+\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]_{2}

with

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]_{0}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}[\mathop{\rm div}(e^{-i\gamma}\varphi)Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+{\rm{curl}}(e^{-i\gamma}\varphi)Q_{\gamma}E_{2}]+\frac{1}{\lambda}w_{\rho}^{2}\mathop{\rm div}(e^{-i\gamma}\varphi))Q_{\gamma}W,

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\cos w\Big[[\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi_{3}\cos\theta+\partial_{x_{2}}\varphi_{3}\sin\theta]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+[\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi_{3}\sin\theta-\partial_{x_{2}}\varphi_{3}\cos\theta]Q_{\gamma}E_{2}\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\sin w[\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi_{3}\cos\theta+\partial_{x_{2}}\varphi_{3}\sin\theta]Q_{\gamma}W,

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}\Big[[\mathop{\rm div}(e^{i\gamma}\bar{\varphi})\cos(2\theta)-{\rm{curl}}(e^{i\gamma}\bar{\varphi})\sin(2\theta)]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+[\mathop{\rm div}(e^{i\gamma}\bar{\varphi})\sin(2\theta)+{\rm{curl}}(e^{i\gamma}\bar{\varphi})\cos(2\theta)]Q_{\gamma}E_{2}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}w_{\rho}\mathop{\rm div}(e^{i\gamma}\bar{\varphi})\cos(2\theta)+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}w_{\rho}{\rm{curl}}(e^{i\gamma}\bar{\varphi})\sin(2\theta)\right]Q_{\gamma}W.

Lemma 2.4
.

For

\Phi(x)=\left[\begin{matrix}\phi(r)e^{i\theta}\\
0\end{matrix}\right]

with
\phi
complex-valued,
the term

\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]

can be expressed as

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}\left[\emph{Re}(e^{-i\gamma}\phi_{r}(r))Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\frac{1}{r}\emph{Im}(e^{-i\gamma}\phi(r))Q_{\gamma}E_{2}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\left(\emph{Re}(\phi_{r}e^{-i\gamma})\cos w-\frac{1}{r}\emph{Re}(\phi e^{-i\gamma})\right)Q_{\gamma}W.

Assume
\psi
is real-valued and

\Psi(x)=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\\
\psi(r)\\
\end{matrix}\right].

Then

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{U}[\Psi]=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\cos w\left(\psi_{r}Q_{\gamma}E_{1}-\frac{1}{r}\psi_{\theta}Q_{\gamma}E_{2}\right)+\frac{2}{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}\psi_{r}Q_{\gamma}W.

The proof of all the lemmas in this section will be postponed to Appendix
A.1
.

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
: Non-degeneracy of the degree
m
bubble

For notational simplicity, we write throughout this section

W^{(m)}=W,\quad E_{1}^{(m)}=E_{1},\quad E_{2}^{(m)}=E_{2}.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We divide the proof into the following steps.

Step 1
. In the polar coordinate system, we write the solution
\phi=\phi(r,\theta)
of (
1.6
) as

\displaystyle\phi(r,\theta)=\xi(r,\theta)E_{1}+\eta(r,\theta)E_{2}+\zeta(r,\theta)W.

Then by direct computation, we know the linearized operator

L[\phi]=\Delta\phi-\frac{2}{r}\phi_{r}\wedge W_{\theta}-\frac{2}{r}W_{r}\wedge\phi_{\theta}:=(L^{(1)}[\phi],L^{(2)}[\phi],L^{(3)}[\phi])

can be expressed as follows,

\displaystyle L^{(1)}[\phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}\left(r^{2m}+1\right)^{3}}\cos(m\theta)\left[-m^{2}(-1+7r^{2m}-7r^{4m}+r^{6m})\xi+16m^{2}r^{3m}\zeta\right.

\displaystyle\quad+(-2m+2mr^{2m}+2mr^{4m}-2mr^{6m})\eta_{\theta}

\displaystyle\quad+(-1-r^{2m}+r^{4m}+r^{6m})\xi_{\theta\theta}+(-r-r^{1+2m}+r^{1+4m}+r^{1+6m})\xi_{r}

\displaystyle\quad+(-r^{2}-r^{2+2m}+r^{2+4m}+r^{2+6m})\xi_{rr}+(2r^{2+m}+4r^{2+3m}+2r^{2+5m})\zeta_{rr}

\displaystyle\quad\left.+(2r^{1+m}+4r^{1+3m}+2r^{1+5m})\zeta_{r}+(2r^{m}+4r^{3m}+2r^{5m})\zeta_{\theta\theta}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(r^{2m}+1)}{r^{2}\left(r^{2m}+1\right)^{3}}\sin(m\theta)\left[(2m-2mr^{4m})\xi_{\theta}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m})\eta\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(-1-2r^{2m}-r^{4m})\eta_{\theta\theta}+(-r-2r^{1+2m}-r^{1+4m})\eta_{r}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left.+(-r-2r^{2+2m}-r^{2+4m})\eta_{rr})\right],

\displaystyle L^{(2)}[\phi]=\frac{-1-r^{2m}}{r^{2}\left(r^{2m}+1\right)^{3}}\cos(m\theta)\left[(2m-2mr^{4m})\xi_{\theta}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m})\eta\right.

\displaystyle\quad+(-1-2r^{2m}-r^{4m})\eta_{\theta\theta}+(-r-2r^{1+2m}-r^{1+4m})\eta_{r}

\displaystyle\quad\left.+(-r^{2}-2r^{2+2m}-r^{2+4m})\eta_{rr}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{r^{2}\left(r^{2m}+1\right)^{3}}\sin(m\theta)\left[-m^{2}(-1+7r^{2m}-7r^{4m}+r^{6m})\xi+16m^{2}r^{3m}\zeta\right.

\displaystyle\quad+(-2m+2mr^{2m}+2mr^{4m}-2mr^{6m})\eta_{\theta}+(-1-r^{2m}+r^{4m}+r^{6m})\xi_{\theta\theta}

\displaystyle\quad+(-r-r^{1+2m}+r^{1+4m}+r^{1+6m})\xi_{r}+(-r^{2}-r^{2+2m}+r^{2+4m}+r^{2+6m})\xi_{rr}

\displaystyle\quad+(2r^{m}+4r^{3m}+2r^{5m})\zeta_{\theta\theta}+(2r^{1+m}+4r^{1+3m}+2r^{1+5m})\zeta_{r}

\displaystyle\quad\left.+(2r^{2+m}+4r^{2+3m}+2r^{2+5m})\zeta_{rr}\right]

and

\displaystyle L^{(3)}[\phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}\left(r^{2m}+1\right)^{3}}\left[2m^{2}(r^{m}-6r^{3m}+r^{5m})\xi+8m^{2}r^{2m}\zeta+(-4mr^{m}+4mr^{5m})\eta_{\theta}\right.

\displaystyle\quad+(-2r^{m}-4r^{3m}-2r^{5m})\xi_{\theta\theta}+(-1-r^{2m}+r^{4m}+r^{6m})\zeta_{\theta\theta}

\displaystyle\quad+(-2r^{1+m}-4r^{1+3m}-2r^{1+5m})\xi_{r}+(-r-r^{1+2m}+r^{1+4m}+r^{1+6m})\zeta_{r}

\displaystyle\quad\left.+(-2r^{2+m}-4r^{2+3m}-2r^{2+5m})\xi_{rr}+(-r^{2}-r^{2+2m}+r^{2+4m}+r^{2+6m})\xi_{rr}\right].

Step 2
. Using Fourier expansion, we set
\xi(r,\theta)=a(r)\cos(k\theta)+b(r)\sin(k\theta)
,
\eta(r,\theta)=c(r)\cos(k\theta)+d(r)\sin(k\theta)
and
\zeta(r,\theta)=e(r)\cos(k\theta)+f(r)\sin(k\theta)
,
k
is an integer, and we write

L_{\perp}(\phi):=(L^{(1)}_{\perp}[\phi],L^{(1)}_{\perp}[\phi],L^{(1)}_{\perp}[\phi]):=(L^{(1)}[\phi],L^{(2)}[\phi],L^{(3)}[\phi])-((L^{(1)}[\phi],L^{(2)}[\phi],L^{(3)}[\phi])\cdot W)W.

Then we have

L^{(1)}_{\perp}[\phi]=\frac{1}{r^{2}\left(r^{2m}+1\right)^{3}}((1+r^{2m})\sin(m\theta)A_{1}+(-1+r^{2m})\cos(m\theta)A_{2})

with

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\cos(k\theta)(-2km(r^{4m}-1)b(r)+(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))c(r))

\displaystyle\quad-\cos(k\theta)(r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(c^{\prime}(r)+rc^{\prime\prime}(r)))

\displaystyle\quad+\sin(k\theta)(2km(r^{4m}-1)a(r)+(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))d(r))

\displaystyle\quad-\sin(k\theta)(r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(d^{\prime}(r)+rd^{\prime\prime}(r))),

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\cos(k\theta)((-k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}-m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))a(r)-2km(r^{4m}-1)d(r))

\displaystyle\quad+\cos(k\theta)(r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(a^{\prime}(r)+ra^{\prime\prime}(r))

\displaystyle\quad+\sin(k\theta)(-k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}-m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))b(r))

\displaystyle\quad+\sin(k\theta)(2km(r^{2m}-1)(r^{2m}+1)c(r)+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(b^{\prime}(r)+rb^{\prime\prime}(r)),

L^{(2)}_{\perp}[\phi]=\frac{1}{r^{2}\left(r^{2m}+1\right)^{3}}((1+r^{2m})\cos(m\theta)B_{1}+(-1+r^{2m})\sin(m\theta)B_{2})

with

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle=\cos(k\theta)(2km(r^{4m}-1)b(r)-(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))c(r))

\displaystyle\quad+\cos(k\theta)(r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(c^{\prime}(r)+rc^{\prime\prime}(r)))

\displaystyle\quad-\sin(k\theta)(2km(r^{4m}-1)a(r)+(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))d(r))

\displaystyle\quad+\sin(k\theta)(r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(d^{\prime}(r)+rd^{\prime\prime}(r))),

\displaystyle B_{2}

\displaystyle=\cos(k\theta)(-(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))a(r)-2km(r^{4m}-1)d(r)

\displaystyle\quad+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(a^{\prime}(r)+ra^{\prime\prime}(r))

\displaystyle\quad+\sin(k\theta)(-(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))b(r)+2km(r^{4m}-1)c(r)

\displaystyle\quad+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(b^{\prime}(r)+rb^{\prime\prime}(r)),

L^{(3)}_{\perp}[\phi]=\frac{2r^{m-2}}{(1+r^{2m})^{3}}\cos(k\theta)C_{1}+\frac{2r^{m-2}}{(1+r^{2m})^{3}}\sin(k\theta)C_{2},

with

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))a(r)+(1+r^{2m})2km(r^{2m}-1)d(r)

\displaystyle-r(1+r^{2m})(a^{\prime}(r)+ra^{\prime\prime}(r)),

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=(k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}+m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))b(r)-(1+r^{2m})2km(r^{2m}-1)c(r)

\displaystyle+r(1+r^{2m})(b^{\prime}(r)+rb^{\prime\prime}(r)).

In the direction of
W
, we have

(L^{(1)}[\phi],L^{(2)}[\phi],L^{(3)}[\phi])\cdot W=\frac{1}{r^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}}\cos(k\theta)D_{1}+\frac{2r^{m-2}}{(1+r^{2m})^{3}}\sin(k\theta)D_{2}

with

D_{1}=-(-8m^{2}r^{2m}+k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2})e(r)+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(e^{\prime}(r)+re^{\prime\prime}(r)),

D_{2}=-(-8m^{2}r^{2m}+k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2})f(r)+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(f^{\prime}(r)+rf^{\prime\prime}(r)).

We also write
L_{\perp}(\phi)
as

\displaystyle L_{\perp}(\phi)

\displaystyle=(L_{\perp}(\phi)\cdot E_{1})E_{1}+(L_{\perp}(\phi)\cdot E_{2})E_{2}

with

\displaystyle L_{\perp}(\phi)\cdot E_{1}

\displaystyle=((-k^{2}(r^{2m}+1)^{2}-m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))a(r)-2km(r^{4m}-1)d(r))\cos(k\theta)

\displaystyle+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(a^{\prime}(r)+ra^{\prime\prime}(r))\cos(k\theta)

\displaystyle+((-k^{2}(r^{2m}+1)^{2}-m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))b(r)+2km(r^{4m}-1)c(r))\sin(k\theta)

\displaystyle+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(b^{\prime}(r)+rb^{\prime\prime}(r))\sin(k\theta)

and

\displaystyle L_{\perp}(\phi)\cdot E_{2}

\displaystyle=((-k^{2}(r^{2m}+1)^{2}-m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))c(r)+2km(r^{4m}-1)b(r))\cos(k\theta)

\displaystyle+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(c^{\prime}(r)+rc^{\prime\prime}(r))\cos(k\theta)

\displaystyle+((-k^{2}(r^{2m}+1)^{2}-m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m}))d(r)-2km(r^{4m}-1)a(r))\sin(k\theta)

\displaystyle+r(1+r^{2m})^{2}(d^{\prime}(r)+rd^{\prime\prime}(r))\sin(k\theta).

Therefore, to solve the linearized equation (
1.6
), we need to solve the following system of ODEs,

(3.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} a^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}a^{\prime}(r)-\frac{k^{2}+\frac{m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m})}{(r^{2m}+1)^{2}}}{r^{2}}a(r)-\frac{2km(r^{2m}-1)}{r^{2}(1+r^{2m})}d(r)=0,\\
d^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}d^{\prime}(r)-\frac{k^{2}+\frac{m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m})}{(r^{2m}+1)^{2}}}{r^{2}}d(r)-\frac{2km(r^{2m}-1)}{r^{2}(1+r^{2m})}a(r)=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} b^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}b^{\prime}(r)-\frac{k^{2}+\frac{m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m})}{(r^{2m}+1)^{2}}}{r^{2}}b(r)+\frac{2km(r^{2m}-1)}{r^{2}(1+r^{2m})}c(r)=0,\\
c^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}c^{\prime}(r)-\frac{k^{2}+\frac{m^{2}(1-6r^{2m}+r^{4m})}{(r^{2m}+1)^{2}}}{r^{2}}c(r)+\frac{2km(r^{2m}-1)}{r^{2}(1+r^{2m})}b(r)=0,\end{aligned}\right.

and

(3.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} e^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}e^{\prime}(r)+\frac{8m^{2}r^{2m}-k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}}{r^{2}(r^{2m}+1)^{2}}e(r)=0,\\
f^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}f^{\prime}(r)+\frac{8m^{2}r^{2m}-k^{2}(1+r^{2m})^{2}}{r^{2}(r^{2m}+1)^{2}}f(r)=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Step 3
. We solve the system (
3.1
)-(
3.3
) in the following four cases.

Case 1
: If
k=0
, then the solutions of (
3.1
)-(
3.3
) are

a(r)=\frac{(c_{1}+c_{3})}{2}\frac{r^{m}}{1+r^{2m}}+\frac{(c_{2}+c_{4})}{2}\frac{2r^{m}\log(r^{2m})+r^{3m}-r^{-m}}{1+r^{2m}},

d(r)=\frac{(c_{1}-c_{3})}{2}\frac{r^{m}}{1+r^{2m}}+\frac{(c_{2}-c_{4})}{2}\frac{2r^{m}\log(r^{2m})+r^{3m}-r^{-m}}{1+r^{2m}},

b(r)=\frac{(c_{5}+c_{7})}{2}\frac{r^{m}}{1+r^{2m}}+\frac{(c_{6}+c_{8})}{2}\frac{2r^{m}\log(r^{2m})+r^{3m}-r^{-m}}{1+r^{2m}},

c(r)=\frac{(c_{5}-c_{7})}{2}\frac{r^{m}}{1+r^{2m}}+\frac{(c_{6}-c_{8})}{2}\frac{2r^{m}\log(r^{2m})+r^{3m}-r^{-m}}{1+r^{2m}},

e(r)=\frac{c_{9}(r^{2m}-1)}{1+r^{2m}}+\frac{c_{10}r^{m}((r^{m}-r^{-m})\log(r^{2m})-4r^{-m})}{1+r^{2m}},

f(r)=\frac{c_{11}(r^{2m}-1)}{1+r^{2m}}+\frac{c_{12}r^{m}((r^{m}-r^{-m})\log(r^{2m})-4r^{-m})}{1+r^{2m}}.

Therefore the bounded kernel functions take the form

\displaystyle\phi(r,\theta)=\frac{(c_{1}+c_{3})}{2}\frac{r^{m}}{1+r^{2m}}\cos(k\theta)E_{1}+\frac{(c_{5}-c_{7})}{2}\frac{r^{m}}{1+r^{2m}}\cos(k\theta)E_{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{9}(r^{2m}-1)}{1+r^{2m}}\cos(k\theta)W.

Case 2
: If
k=1
, then the solutions of (
3.1
)-(
3.3
) are

\displaystyle a(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{13}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{14}r((-1-m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}+mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{15}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{16}r^{-1}((-1+m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}-mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle d(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{13}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{14}r((-1-m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}+mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{15}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-\frac{c_{16}r^{-1}((-1+m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}-mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle b(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{17}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{18}r((-1-m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}+mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{19}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{20}r^{-1}((-1+m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}-mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle c(r)

\displaystyle=-\frac{c_{17}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-\frac{c_{18}r((-1-m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}+mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{19}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{20}r^{-1}((-1+m)r^{-m}+2m^{2}r^{m}-mr^{3m}-2r^{m}-r^{3m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle e(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{21}}{1+r^{2m}}((1-m)r^{-m}+(1+m)r^{m})r^{m-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{22}}{1+r^{2m}}((1+m)r^{-m}+(1-m)r^{m})r^{m+1},

\displaystyle f(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{23}}{1+r^{2m}}((1-m)r^{-m}+(1+m)r^{m})r^{m-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{24}}{1+r^{2m}}((1+m)r^{-m}+(1-m)r^{m})r^{m+1}.

If
m=1
, the bounded kernel functions take the form

\displaystyle\phi(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\frac{c_{13}}{2(1+r^{2})}+\frac{c_{15}r^{2}}{2(1+r^{2})}\right)\cos(\theta)+\left(\frac{c_{17}}{2(1+r^{2})}+\frac{c_{19}r^{2}}{2(1+r^{2})}\right)\sin(\theta)\right)E_{1}

\displaystyle+\left(\left(-\frac{c_{17}}{2(1+r^{2})}+\frac{c_{19}r^{2}}{2(1+r^{2})}\right)\cos(\theta)+\left(\frac{c_{13}}{2(1+r^{2})}-\frac{c_{15}r^{2}}{2(1+r^{2})}\right)\sin(\theta)\right)E_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{(c_{21}+c_{22})2r}{1+r^{2}}\cos(\theta)+\frac{(c_{23}+c_{24})2r}{1+r^{2}}\sin(\theta)\right)W.

If
m>1
, the bounded kernel functions take the form

\displaystyle\phi(r,\theta)=

\displaystyle\left(\left(\frac{c_{13}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{15}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\cos(\theta)+\left(\frac{c_{17}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{19}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\sin(\theta)\right)E_{1}

\displaystyle+\left(\left(-\frac{c_{17}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{19}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\cos(\theta)+\left(\frac{c_{13}r^{m-1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-\frac{c_{15}r^{m+1}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\sin(\theta)\right)E_{2}.

Case 3
: If
k=m
, then the solutions of (
3.1
)-(
3.3
) are

\displaystyle a(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{25}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{26}r^{m}(r^{3m}+2r^{-m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{27}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{28}r^{-m}(-2r^{3m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m}+r^{-m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle d(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{25}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{26}r^{m}(r^{3m}+2r^{-m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{27}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-\frac{c_{28}r^{-m}(-2r^{3m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m}+r^{-m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle b(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{29}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{30}r^{-m}(-2r^{3m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m}+r^{-m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{31}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{32}r^{m}(r^{3m}+2r^{-m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle c(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{29}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{30}r^{-m}(-2r^{3m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m}+r^{-m})}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{31}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-\frac{c_{32}r^{m}(r^{3m}+2r^{-m}\log(r^{2m})+4r^{m})}{2(1+r^{2m})},

e(r)=\frac{c_{33}r^{m}}{(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{34}(2r^{m}\log(r^{2m})+r^{3m}-r^{-m})}{1+r^{2m}},

f(r)=\frac{c_{35}r^{m}}{(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{36}(2r^{m}\log(r^{2m})+r^{3m}-r^{-m})}{1+r^{2m}}.

Therefore the bounded kernel functions take the form

\displaystyle\phi(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\frac{c_{25}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{27}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\cos(k\theta)+\left(\frac{c_{29}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+\frac{c_{31}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\sin(k\theta)\right)E_{1}

\displaystyle+\left(\left(\frac{c_{29}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-\frac{c_{31}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\cos(k\theta)+\left(\frac{c_{25}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-\frac{c_{27}r^{2m}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\sin(k\theta)\right)E_{2}

\displaystyle+(\frac{c_{33}r^{m}}{(1+r^{2m})}\cos(k\theta)+\frac{c_{34}r^{m}}{(1+r^{2m})}\sin(k\theta))W.

Case 4
: The solutions of (
3.1
)-(
3.3
) in the case
k\neq 0
,
k\neq 1
and
k\neq m
are

\displaystyle a(r)

\displaystyle=c_{37}\frac{r^{m-k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+c_{38}\frac{r^{k}(\frac{k(k+m)}{2}r^{-m}+(k-m)(kr^{3m}/2+(k+m)r^{m}))}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle+c_{39}\frac{r^{m+k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+c_{40}\frac{r^{-k}(\frac{k(k-m)}{2}r^{-m}+(k+m)(kr^{3m}/2+(k-m)r^{m}))}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle d(r)

\displaystyle=c_{37}\frac{r^{m-k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+c_{38}\frac{r^{k}(\frac{k(k+m)}{2}r^{-m}+(k-m)(kr^{3m}/2+(k+m)r^{m}))}{2(1+r^{2m})}

\displaystyle-c_{39}\frac{r^{m+k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-c_{40}\frac{r^{-k}(\frac{k(k-m)}{2}r^{-m}+(k+m)(kr^{3m}/2+(k-m)r^{m}))}{2(1+r^{2m})},

\displaystyle b(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{-m-k}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(c_{41}(1+(k+m)r^{2m}(\frac{2}{k}+\frac{r^{2m}}{k-m}))+c_{42}r^{2(k+m)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+c_{43}\frac{r^{2k}(k(k+m)+2(k^{2}-m^{2})r^{2m}+k(k-m)r^{4m})}{k(k+m)}+c_{44}r^{2m}\right),

\displaystyle c(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{-m-k}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(c_{41}(1+(k+m)r^{2m}(\frac{2}{k}+\frac{r^{2m}}{k-m}))+c_{42}r^{2(k+m)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-c_{43}\frac{r^{2k}(k(k+m)+2(k^{2}-m^{2})r^{2m}+k(k-m)r^{4m})}{k(k+m)}-c_{44}r^{2m}\right),

\displaystyle e(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{45}}{1+r^{2m}}((k-m)r^{-m}+(k+m)r^{m})r^{m-k}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{46}}{1+r^{2m}}((k+m)r^{-m}+(k-m)r^{m})r^{m+k},

\displaystyle f(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{47}}{1+r^{2m}}((k-m)r^{-m}+(k+m)r^{m})r^{m-k}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{48}}{1+r^{2m}}((k+m)r^{-m}+(k-m)r^{m})r^{m+k}.

If
k>m
, there are no bounded kernel functions. If
k=2,\cdots,m-1
, the bounded kernel functions take the form

\displaystyle\phi(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\left(c_{37}\frac{r^{m-k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}+c_{39}\frac{r^{m+k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\cos(k\theta)E_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{r^{-m-k}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(c_{42}r^{2(k+m)}+c_{44}r^{2m}\right)\sin(k\theta)E_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{r^{-m-k}}{1+r^{2m}}\left(c_{42}r^{2(k+m)}-c_{44}r^{2m}\right)\cos(k\theta)E_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(c_{37}\frac{r^{m-k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}-c_{39}\frac{r^{m+k}}{2(1+r^{2m})}\right)\sin(k\theta)E_{2}.

From the above computations, we conclude the validity of Theorem
1.1
.

∎

4.
Approximation and improvement

In this section, we find a suitable profile that approximates well the real solution to (
1.9
) and then further improve it by adding several corrections. We first define the error of
u
as

(4.1)

S[u]:=-u_{t}+\Delta u-2u_{x_{1}}\wedge u_{x_{2}}.

Recall (
2.2
). We take the approximate solution as

(4.2)

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma(t)}\left[W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)+c_{1}(t)\frac{2\rho}{\rho^{2}+1}\cos\theta W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)+c_{2}(t)\frac{2\rho}{\rho^{2}+1}\sin\theta W\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}(W+c_{1}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}+c_{2}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}),

where
Q_{\gamma}
in (
1.12
) can be written as

Q_{\gamma}=e^{\gamma J_{z}},\quad J_{z}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix},

and the modulation parameters
\lambda(t),~\xi(t),~\gamma(t),~c_{1}(t),~c_{2}(t)
are to be determined.

Remark 4.0.1
.

By the non-degeneracy of degree
1

H
-bubble, there are nine kernels for the associated linearized operator. In the above ansatz, we only introduce six modulation parameters. The other three correspond to the infinitesimal generators of rigid motions: rotations around
x
- and
y
-axes, and another translation for the map in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and the kernels are all of order one at space infinity. For technical reasons, we take advantage of regularity properties to control these modes in the absence of modulation.

We have

(4.3)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\partial}_{\rho}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}=\cos\theta w_{\rho}(\cos wW+\sin wE_{1}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\partial}_{\theta}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}=-\sin w\sin\theta W+\sin^{2}w\cos\theta E_{2},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\partial}_{\rho}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}=\sin\theta w_{\rho}(\cos wW+\sin wE_{1}),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\partial}_{\theta}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}=\sin w\cos\theta W+\sin^{2}w\sin\theta E_{2}.

Then the error of the approximation (
4.2
) is given by

\displaystyle~S[U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~-{\partial}_{t}(Q_{\gamma}W)-\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left[\dot{c}_{j}Q_{\gamma}\mathcal{Z}_{1,j}+c_{j}{\partial}_{t}(Q_{\gamma}\mathcal{Z}_{1,j})\right]

\displaystyle~-2c_{1}c_{2}Q_{\gamma}{\partial}_{x_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}\wedge{\partial}_{x_{2}}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}-2c_{1}c_{2}Q_{\gamma}{\partial}_{x_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}\wedge{\partial}_{x_{2}}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~-{\partial}_{t}(Q_{\gamma}W)-\sum_{j=1}^{2}\dot{c}_{j}Q_{\gamma}\mathcal{Z}_{1,j}-\sum_{j=1}^{2}c_{j}\dot{\gamma}J_{z}e^{\gamma J_{z}}\mathcal{Z}_{1,j}-\sum_{j=1}^{2}c_{j}Q_{\gamma}({\partial}_{\rho}\mathcal{Z}_{1,j}\rho_{t}+{\partial}_{\theta}\mathcal{Z}_{1,j}\theta_{t})

\displaystyle~-\frac{2\lambda^{-2}c_{1}c_{2}}{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left({\partial}_{\rho}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}\wedge{\partial}_{\theta}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}+{\partial}_{\rho}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2}\wedge{\partial}_{\theta}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\dot{\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle~+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\xi}_{1}w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left[\cos\theta E_{1}+\sin\theta E_{2}\right]+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\xi}_{2}w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left[\sin\theta E_{1}-\cos\theta E_{2}\right]

\displaystyle~-\frac{2\rho}{1+\rho^{2}}(\dot{c}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{c}_{2}\sin\theta)Q_{\gamma}W-\frac{2\dot{\gamma}\rho\sin w}{1+\rho^{2}}\left(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta\right)Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle~-\bigg[\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)(c_{2}\cos\theta-c_{1}\sin\theta)

\displaystyle~\quad-\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}\cos w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta+\dot{\lambda}\rho)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}W

\displaystyle~+\bigg[\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta+\dot{\lambda}\rho)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}

\displaystyle~-\bigg[\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}\sin^{2}w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle~-\frac{2\lambda^{-2}c_{1}c_{2}}{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left(-\sin 2\theta w_{\rho}\sin^{2}w\cos wE_{1}-\cos 2\theta w_{\rho}\sin^{2}wE_{2}+\sin 2\theta w_{\rho}\sin^{3}wW\right),

where we have used (
4.3
). We then arrange terms and write

(4.4)

\displaystyle S[U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\dot{\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}E_{2}}_{:=\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}}~+~\mathcal{R}_{U^{\perp}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{(-2)\lambda^{-2}c_{1}c_{2}w_{\rho}^{2}\sin w(\sin 2\theta\cos wQ_{\gamma}E_{1}+\cos 2\theta Q_{\gamma}E_{2})}_{:=\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{\dot{\xi}_{1}}{\lambda}w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left[\cos\theta E_{1}+\sin\theta E_{2}\right]+\frac{\dot{\xi}_{2}}{\lambda}w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left[\sin\theta E_{1}-\cos\theta E_{2}\right]}_{:=\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\rho w_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]Q_{\gamma}W}_{:=\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{2\lambda^{-2}c_{1}c_{2}\sin 2\theta\rho w_{\rho}^{2}\sin^{2}wQ_{\gamma}W}_{:=\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 2)}}~+~\mathcal{R}_{U},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{U^{\perp}}:=

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}\sin w\left(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta\right)Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle+\bigg[\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta+\dot{\lambda}\rho)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}

\displaystyle-\bigg[\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}\sin^{2}w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg[\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta+\dot{\lambda}\rho)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}

\displaystyle+\bigg[\Big(\dot{\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}\sin w-\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}\sin^{2}w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)\Big)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{U}:=

\displaystyle-\bigg[\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)(c_{2}\cos\theta-c_{1}\sin\theta)

\displaystyle-\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}[\cos w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta)+\dot{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}](c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}W.

We observe that, compared to
\mathcal{R}_{U^{\perp}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{U}
, the error terms
\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}
decay slower in space, and
\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 2)}
decay slower in time (in view of the blow-up dynamics that we search for). So we add several global corrections to improve the errors
\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}
,
\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}
,
\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 2)}
.

To deal with
\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}
, we add two global/non-local corrections to improve the spatial decay at leading order. We aim to find
\Phi^{(0)}
and
\Phi^{(1)}
solving approximately

\displaystyle~{\partial}_{t}\Phi^{(0)}\approx\Delta\Phi^{(0)}+\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)},

\displaystyle~{\partial}_{t}\Phi^{(1)}\approx\Delta\Phi^{(1)}+\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}.

Here
\Phi^{(0)}=\Phi^{(0)}[\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}]
and
\Phi^{(1)}=\Phi^{(1)}[\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}]
are non-local corrections, depending on the choice of parameters, to be specified below.
To handle
\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 2)}
, we solve the following two linearized problems:

\displaystyle~{\partial}_{t}\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}=L_{U}[\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}]+\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)},\quad j=\pm 2,

\displaystyle~{\partial}_{t}\Phi^{(2)}_{U}=L_{U}[\Phi^{(2)}_{U}]+\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 2)},

where
\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}=\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}(\rho,t)
is on
U^{\perp}
, and
\Phi^{(2)}_{U}=\Phi^{(2)}_{U}(\rho,t)
is in the
U
-direction.

\bullet

Approximate form of
\Phi^{(0)}
.

We notice that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}=

\displaystyle~\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\dot{\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle~-\frac{2r(\dot{\lambda}+i\lambda\dot{\gamma})}{\lambda^{2}+r^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(\theta+\gamma)}\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}~\mbox{ for }~r=|x-\xi|\gg\lambda.

So we assume that

\Phi^{(0)}(x,t)=\left[\begin{matrix}\varphi^{0}(x,t)\\
0\end{matrix}\right]

and
\varphi^{0}
is an approximation of

\varphi^{0}_{t}=\Delta\varphi^{0}-\frac{2r}{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}}(\dot{\lambda}+i\lambda\dot{\gamma})e^{i(\theta+\gamma)}.

Let

\varphi^{0}(x,t)=re^{i\theta}\psi^{0}(z(r),t),\quad z(r)=\sqrt{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}(t)},

for
\psi^{0}(z,t)
satisfying the equation

\psi^{0}_{t}=\psi^{0}_{zz}+\frac{3\psi^{0}_{z}}{z}+\frac{p_{0}(t)}{z^{2}},

where we define

(4.5)

p_{0}(t):=-2(\dot{\lambda}+i\lambda\dot{\gamma})e^{i\gamma}.

From the Duhamel’s principle, we know that

(4.6)

\psi^{0}(z,t)=\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)k(z,t-s)ds,\quad k(z,t)=\frac{1-e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{4t}}}{z^{2}}

is a weak solution. Notice
k_{t}=k_{zz}+\frac{3}{z}k_{z}
. Then we have

-\partial_{t}\Phi^{(0)}+\Delta_{x}\Phi^{(0)}=\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{0}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{0}:=\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{0},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{0}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathcal{R}_{0}^{0}\\
0\end{matrix}\right],\quad\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{0}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{0}\\
0\end{matrix}\right]

where

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{0}=-re^{i\theta}\frac{p_{0}(t)}{z^{2}}+re^{i\theta}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds

and

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{0}=e^{i\theta}{\rm Re}[(\dot{\xi}e^{-i\theta})]\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)k(z(r),t-s)ds-\frac{r}{z^{2}}e^{i\theta}\left(\lambda\dot{\lambda}-{\rm Re}(re^{-i\theta}\dot{\xi}(t))\right)\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)zk_{z}(z(r),t-s)ds.

\bullet

Approximate form of
\Phi^{(1)}
.

Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}=

\displaystyle~\rho w_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]Q_{\gamma}W

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle~-\frac{2r}{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
\cos\theta(\lambda c_{1})^{\prime}+\sin\theta(\lambda c_{2})^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}~\mbox{ for }~r\gg\lambda,

we assume that

\Phi^{(1)}(x,t)=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\\
{\rm Re}(\varphi^{1}(x,t))\end{matrix}\right]

and
\varphi^{1}
solves approximately

\varphi^{1}_{t}=\Delta\varphi^{1}-\frac{2r}{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}}e^{-i\theta}(\lambda\mathbf{c})^{\prime},

where we use the notation

(4.7)

{\mathbf{c}}(t):=c_{1}(t)+ic_{2}(t).

We look for

\varphi^{1}(x,t)=re^{-i\theta}\psi^{1}(z(r),t),\quad z(r)=\sqrt{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}},

where
\psi^{1}(z,t)
satisfies the equation

\psi^{1}_{t}=\psi^{1}_{zz}+\frac{3\psi^{1}_{z}}{z}+\frac{p_{1}(t)}{z^{2}},

and here we define

(4.8)

\quad p_{1}(t):=-2(\lambda{\mathbf{c}})^{\prime}.

Similar to (
4.6
), we see that

(4.9)

\psi^{1}(z,t)=\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)k(z,t-s)ds.

So we add a correction

\Phi^{(1)}(x,t)=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\\
{\rm Re}(\varphi^{1}(r,t))\end{matrix}\right]

with

\varphi^{1}(r,t)=re^{-i\theta}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)k(z(r),t-s)ds.

We next compute the new error produced by
\Phi^{(1)}
:

-\partial_{t}\Phi^{(1)}+\Delta_{x}\Phi^{(1)}={\rm Re}(\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{1}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{1}):=\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{1}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\\
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{1}\end{matrix}\right],\quad\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{1}=\left[\begin{matrix}0\\
0\\
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{1}\end{matrix}\right]

where

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{1}=-re^{-i\theta}\frac{p_{1}(t)}{z^{2}}+re^{-i\theta}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds

and

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{1}=e^{-i\theta}{\rm Re}[(\dot{\xi}e^{-i\theta})]\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)k(z(r),t-s)ds-\frac{re^{-i\theta}}{z^{2}}\left(\lambda\dot{\lambda}-{{\rm Re}}(re^{-i\theta}\dot{\xi}(t))\right)\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)zk_{z}(z(r),t-s)ds.

\bullet

Equations for
\Phi^{(\pm 2)}_{U^{\perp}}
.

To handle
\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)}
in (
4.4
), we first write it in complex form:

\displaystyle\left(Q_{-\gamma}\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)}\right)_{\mathbb{C}}=

\displaystyle~-2\lambda^{-2}c_{1}c_{2}w^{2}_{\rho}\sin w(\sin 2\theta\cos w+i\cos 2\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~-i\lambda^{-2}c_{1}c_{2}w^{2}_{\rho}\sin w\left[e^{2i\theta}(1-\cos w)+e^{-2i\theta}(1+\cos w)\right]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle~\left(Q_{-\gamma}\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(2)}\right)_{\mathbb{C}}+\left(Q_{-\gamma}\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(-2)}\right)_{\mathbb{C}}.

We try to add two corrections, expressed in
(\rho,t)
coordinates

(4.10)

\displaystyle\Phi^{(-2)}_{U^{\perp}}(\rho,t)=\phi^{(-2)}_{1}Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\phi^{(-2)}_{2}Q_{\gamma}E_{2},\quad(\Phi_{U^{\perp}}^{(-2)})_{\mathbb{C}}=\phi^{(-2)}_{1}+i\phi^{(-2)}_{2}:=\varphi_{-2}(\rho,t)e^{-2i\theta}

\displaystyle\Phi_{U^{\perp}}^{(2)}(\rho,t)=\phi^{(2)}_{1}Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\phi^{(2)}_{2}Q_{\gamma}E_{2},\quad(\Phi_{U^{\perp}}^{(2)})_{\mathbb{C}}=\phi^{(2)}_{1}+i\phi^{(2)}_{2}:=\varphi_{2}(\rho,t)e^{2i\theta},

where the complex-valued
\varphi_{-2}
and
\varphi_{2}
solve

(4.11)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}{\partial}_{t}\varphi_{j}={\partial}_{\rho\rho}\varphi_{j}+\frac{1}{\rho}{\partial}_{\rho}\varphi_{j}+\left[\frac{8}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}}-\frac{1+j^{2}}{\rho^{2}}-\frac{2j(\rho^{2}-1)}{\rho^{2}(\rho^{2}+1)}\right]\varphi_{j}+\lambda^{2}e^{-ji\theta}\left(Q_{-\gamma}\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(j)}\right)_{\mathbb{C}},\quad j=\pm 2.

In other words, we solve the linearized problem

\displaystyle~{\partial}_{t}\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}=L_{U}[\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}]+\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(\pm 2)},\quad j=\pm 2,

where
\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}=\Phi^{(j)}_{U^{\perp}}(\rho,t)
is on
U^{\perp}
. A solution to (
4.11
) with zero initial data will be ensured by a linear theory developed later in Section
7.2
.

\bullet

Equation for
\Phi^{(2)}_{U}
.

To deal with
\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 2)}
given in (
4.4
), we need a correction
\Phi^{(2)}_{U}(\rho,t)
in the form

(4.12)

\Phi^{(2)}_{U}(\rho,t)=\sin 2\theta\psi_{2}(\rho,t)Q_{\gamma}W

with
\psi_{2}
solving

(4.13)

\lambda^{2}{\partial}_{t}\psi_{2}={\partial}_{\rho\rho}\psi_{2}+\frac{1}{\rho}{\partial}_{\rho}\psi_{2}-\frac{4}{\rho^{2}}\psi_{2}+\frac{8}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}}\psi_{2}+2c_{1}c_{2}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}\sin^{2}w

since it is exactly mode
\pm 2
of linearization in the
U
-direction (cf. (
2.5
)). Equation (
4.13
) with zero Cauchy data will be solved by the linear theory in Section
7.2
.

We now compute the new error
S[U_{*}]
of the corrected approximation

(4.14)

\displaystyle U_{*}:=

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,\gamma,\xi,c_{1},c_{2}}+\eta_{1}\Big(\Phi^{(0)}+\Phi^{(1)}+\Phi^{(2)}_{U^{\perp}}+\Phi^{(-2)}_{U^{\perp}}+\Phi^{(2)}_{U}\Big)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}(W+c_{1}\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}+c_{2}\mathcal{Z}_{1,2})+\eta_{1}\Phi_{*},

where the purpose of the cut-off function

\eta_{1}:=\eta(x-\xi(t))

is to avoid potential slow spatial decay in the remote region. Here
\eta(s)
is a smooth cut-off function with
\eta(s)=1
for
s<1
and
\eta(s)=0
for
s>2
.
We first analyze the leading terms

\mathcal{R}_{U^{\perp}}+\mathcal{R}_{U}+\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)}+\eta_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{0})+\eta_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1})+\eta_{1}\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi^{(0)}+\Phi^{(1)}]

in the corrected error
S[U_{*}]
. By definition, we have

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{U^{\perp}}+\mathcal{R}_{U}+\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg[\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta+\dot{\lambda}\rho)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}

\displaystyle+\bigg[\Big(\dot{\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}\sin w-\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}\sin^{2}w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)\Big)(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle-\bigg[\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)(c_{2}\cos\theta-c_{1}\sin\theta)

\displaystyle-\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}[\cos w(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta)+\dot{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}](c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)\bigg]Q_{\gamma}W

\displaystyle+\frac{\dot{\xi}_{1}}{\lambda}w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left[\cos\theta E_{1}+\sin\theta E_{2}\right]+\frac{\dot{\xi}_{2}}{\lambda}w_{\rho}Q_{\gamma}\left[\sin\theta E_{1}-\cos\theta E_{2}\right],

whose complex form on
W^{\perp}
is

(4.16)

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{R}_{U^{\perp}}+\mathcal{R}_{U}+\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)}\Big)_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho w_{\rho}\sin w(\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}+i\dot{\gamma})(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)

\displaystyle+\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}\sin w(\dot{\xi}_{1}-i\dot{\xi}_{2})e^{i\theta}(c_{1}\cos\theta+c_{2}\sin\theta)

\displaystyle+\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}(\dot{\xi}_{1}-i\dot{\xi}_{2})e^{i\theta}.

Next, by using

(4.17)

\displaystyle\left[\begin{matrix}f(\rho)e^{i\theta}\\
g(\rho,\theta)\\
\end{matrix}\right]=

\displaystyle\Big[\cos w{\rm Re}(fe^{-i\gamma})-g\sin w\Big]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+{\rm Im}(fe^{-i\gamma})Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle+\Big[\sin w{\rm Re}(fe^{-i\gamma})+g\cos w\Big]Q_{\gamma}W,

one has

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho w_{\rho}\begin{bmatrix}(\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}+i\dot{\gamma})e^{i(\theta+\gamma)}\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{R}_{0}^{0}\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{0}\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}+\rho w_{\rho}\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(\theta+\gamma)}w_{\rho}\\
-\sin w\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}(r\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds)e^{i\theta}\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}(\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}e^{i\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}^{2})e^{i\theta}\\
\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}\rho^{2}w_{\rho}^{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{0}\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg[\cos w{\rm Re}[(f_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}+f_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)})e^{-i\gamma}]-\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}\rho^{2}w_{\rho}^{2}\sin w\Bigg]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+{\rm Im}[(f_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}+f_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)})e^{-i\gamma}]Q_{\gamma}E_{2}

\displaystyle+\Big[\sin w{\rm Re}[(f_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}+f_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)})e^{-i\gamma}]+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}\rho^{2}w_{\rho}^{2}\cos w\Big]Q_{\gamma}W,

where

(4.19)

\displaystyle f_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}:=

\displaystyle r\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z,t-s)ds+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}e^{i\gamma}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}-\frac{r}{z^{2}}\lambda\dot{\lambda}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)zk_{z}(z,t-s)ds,

\displaystyle f_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)}:=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Re}[(\dot{\xi}e^{-i\theta})]\int_{-T}^{t}p_{0}(s)\Big(k(z,t-s)+\frac{r^{2}}{z}k_{z}(z,t-s)\Big)ds.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho w_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(\theta+\gamma)}\sin w\\
w_{\rho}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle+\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
{\rm Re}\left[re^{-i\theta}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds\right]\\
\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
{\rm Re}[\mathcal{R}_{1}^{1}]\\
\end{bmatrix},

and from (
4.17
), it follows that

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}\Big)\cdot Q_{\gamma}W=

\displaystyle~-\sin w\left(\rho^{2}w^{2}_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]\right)

\displaystyle~+\left(\rho w^{2}_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]\right)\cos w

\displaystyle~+{\rm Re}\left[re^{-i\theta}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds+\mathcal{R}_{1}^{1}\right]\cos w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~-\rho w^{2}_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]

\displaystyle~+{\rm Re}\left[re^{-i\theta}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds+\mathcal{R}_{1}^{1}\right]\cos w,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}\Big)\cdot Q_{\gamma}E_{1}=

\displaystyle~-\cos w\left(\rho^{2}w^{2}_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]\right)

\displaystyle~-\left(\rho w^{2}_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]\right)\sin w

\displaystyle~-{\rm Re}\left[re^{-i\theta}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds+\mathcal{R}_{1}^{1}\right]\sin w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~-\rho^{2}w^{2}_{\rho}\left[\cos\theta(\dot{c}_{1}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{1})+\sin\theta(\dot{c}_{2}+\lambda^{-1}\dot{\lambda}c_{2})\right]

\displaystyle~-{\rm Re}\left[re^{-i\theta}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{z^{4}}\int_{-T}^{t}p_{1}(s)(zk_{z}-z^{2}k_{zz})(z(r),t-s)ds+\mathcal{R}_{1}^{1}\right]\sin w,

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1}\Big)\cdot Q_{\gamma}E_{2}=

\displaystyle~0.

Also, from Lemma
2.4
, we have

\displaystyle~\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi^{(0)}+\Phi^{(1)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{2}{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}\left[{\rm Re}(e^{-i\gamma}(\psi^{0}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}{\partial}_{z}\psi^{0}))Q_{\gamma}E_{1}+\frac{1}{r}{\rm Im}(e^{-i\gamma}r\psi^{0})Q_{\gamma}E_{2}\right]

\displaystyle~+\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\left({\rm Re}(e^{-i\gamma}(\psi^{0}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}{\partial}_{z}\psi^{0}))\cos w-\frac{1}{r}{\rm Re}(e^{-i\gamma}r\psi^{0})\right)Q_{\gamma}W

\displaystyle~-\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\cos w\left({\rm Re}[e^{-i\theta}(\psi^{1}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}\psi_{z}^{1})]Q_{\gamma}E_{1}-\frac{1}{r}{\rm Im}[e^{-i\theta}r\psi^{1}]Q_{\gamma}E_{2}\right)

(4.21)

\displaystyle~+\frac{2}{\lambda}\rho w_{\rho}^{2}{\rm Re}[e^{-i\theta}(\psi^{1}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}\psi_{z}^{1})]Q_{\gamma}W

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\left({\rm Re}(e^{-i\gamma}(\psi^{0}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}{\partial}_{z}\psi^{0}))\cos w-\frac{1}{r}{\rm Re}(e^{-i\gamma}r\psi^{0})+\rho w_{\rho}{\rm Re}[e^{-i\theta}(\psi^{1}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}\psi_{z}^{1})]\right)Q_{\gamma}W

\displaystyle~+\frac{2}{\lambda}w_{\rho}\left(\rho w_{\rho}{\rm Re}(e^{-i\gamma}(\psi^{0}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}{\partial}_{z}\psi^{0}))-\cos w{\rm Re}[e^{-i\theta}(\psi^{1}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}\psi_{z}^{1})]\right)Q_{\gamma}E_{1}

\displaystyle~+\frac{2}{\lambda r}w_{\rho}\left(\rho w_{\rho}{\rm Im}(e^{-i\gamma}r\psi^{0})+\cos w{\rm Im}[e^{-i\theta}r\psi^{1}]\right)Q_{\gamma}E_{2}.

We denote the remaining terms in the error
S[U_{*}]
by

(4.22)

\mathcal{R}_{*}:=S[U_{*}]-\Big[\mathcal{R}_{U^{\perp}}+\mathcal{R}_{U}+\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(1)}+\eta_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{U^{\perp}}^{(0)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{0})+\eta_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{U}^{(\pm 1)}+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1})+\eta_{1}\tilde{L}_{U}[\Phi^{(0)}+\Phi^{(1)}]\Big]-E_{\eta_{1}},

where
E_{\eta_{1}}
is defined in (
A.2
),
and we claim:

Lemma 4.1
.

The remainder
\mathcal{R}_{*}
in the corrected error
S[U_{*}]
projected in each direction
Q_{\gamma}W
,
Q_{\gamma}E_{1}
and
Q_{\gamma}E_{2}
is given by

\displaystyle~\mathcal{R}_{*}\cdot Q_{\gamma}W

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\eta_{1}\lambda^{-1}w_{\rho}(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta+\dot{\lambda}\rho)(\phi_{1}^{(2)}+\phi_{1}^{(-2)})

\displaystyle~+\eta_{1}\sin w[\dot{\gamma}+\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)](\phi_{2}^{(2)}+\phi_{2}^{(-2)})

\displaystyle~+\eta_{1}\lambda^{-1}(\dot{\xi}_{1}\cos\theta+\dot{\xi}_{2}\sin\theta+\dot{\lambda}\rho)\sin 2\theta{\partial}_{\rho}\psi_{2}

\displaystyle~-2\eta_{1}\lambda^{-1}\rho^{-1}(\dot{\xi}_{1}\sin\theta-\dot{\xi}_{2}\cos\theta)\cos 2\theta\psi_{2}

\displaystyle~-\frac{2\eta_{1}^{2}}{r}\Bigg[\left(\cos w{\rm Re}\left[e^{-i\gamma}(\psi^{0}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}{\partial}_{z}\psi^{0})\right]-{\rm Re}[e^{-i\theta}(\psi^{1}+\frac{r^{2}}{z}{\partial}_{z}\psi^{1})]\sin w\right)

\displaystyle~\qquad+\lambda^{-1}\left(\sin 2\theta w_{\rho}\psi_{2}+{\partial}_{\rho}\phi_{1}^{(2)}+{\partial}_{\rho}\phi_{1}^{(-2)}\right)\Bigg]
